On the Weierstraß form of infinite-dimensional differential algebraic equations
Authors: Mehmet Erbay, Birgit Jacob, Kirsten Morris
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
The solvability for infinite-dimensional differential algebraic equations possessing a resolvent index and a Weierstraß form is studied. In particular, the concept of integrated semigroups is used to determine a subset on which solutions exist and are unique. This information is later used for a important class of systems, namely, port-Hamiltonian differential algebraic equations.

Introduction

Linear differential algebraic equations (DAEs), sometimes also called descriptor systems or implicit differential equations, arise in various fields such as physics, engineering and economics. They can be written as1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\hbox {d}}{{\hbox {d}}t}Ex(t)=Ax(t), \quad t\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtEx(t)=Ax(t),t≥0.Here, for complex Hilbert spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}Z, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E:\mathcal {X}\rightarrow \mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}E:X→Z is a bounded operator (denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\in L(\mathcal {X},\mathcal {Z})$$\end{document}E∈L(X,Z)) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A:\mathcal {D}(A)\subseteq \mathcal {X} \rightarrow \mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}A:D(A)⊆X→Z is a closed and densely defined operator. We will often write (E, A) to refer to (1).

In comparison with ordinary differential equations, DAEs include both algebraic and differential constraints. Because of this, in general E has a non-trivial kernel. In finite dimensions it is always possible to decouple the system into an algebraic and a differential part by transforming (1) into a so-called Weierstraß form2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\hbox {d}}{{\hbox {d}}t}\begin{bmatrix} I & 0 \\ 0 & N \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x_1(t)\\ x_2(t)\end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} J & 0 \\ 0 & I\end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x_1(t) \\ x_2(t)\end{bmatrix}, \quad t\ge 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtI00Nx1(t)x2(t)=J00Ix1(t)x2(t),t≥0,where J is in Jordan form and N is nilpotent, as long as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\lambda E-A)^{-1} \in L(\mathcal {Z},\mathcal {X})$$\end{document}(λE-A)-1∈L(Z,X) for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}λ∈C, [11, Ch. 2.1]. In this context, the nilpotency degree of N is called the nilpotency index of (E, A) [2, 11]. For finite-dimensional spaces, this definition of index coincides with other common definitions of the index. In this context, investigation of the different index definitions can be found in [5, 22, 23] for the resolvent index, [7, 19] for the radiality index, [2] for a general comparison of different known indices in infinite dimensions and [11, 12] for the finite-dimensional case.

However, in infinite dimensions the existence of a Weierstraß form is not guaranteed, nor is there a general procedure for calculating it. In view of this difficulty, we will specify a condition under which such a form always exists, namely the existence of the radiality index. One of the main uses of the Weierstraß form is to analyse the well-posedness of the system, since the DAE is divided into an algebraic part and an ODE part, see (2). For the ODE part it is possible to use known solution methods. The solvability of infinite-dimensional DAEs has been intensively studied; see for example, [5, 7, 13, 14, 17, 18, 20–23]. In [20] the splitting of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{ker}\, E$$\end{document}kerE and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{ran}\, E^*$$\end{document}ranE∗ and the restriction of the DAE to the factorized space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X} / \textrm{ker}\, E$$\end{document}X/kerE is studied, in [7, 19] and [21] the splitting of the space, given that the radiality index exists, and the growth of the pseudo-resolvents \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\lambda E-A)^{-1} E$$\end{document}(λE-A)-1E and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E(\lambda E-A)^{-1}$$\end{document}E(λE-A)-1 is analysed, in [5] a more general observation of the pseudo-resolvent growth is provided and a useful dissipativity condition for solving the DAE is presented, in [22] and [23] with the use of Wong-sequences and the resolvent index a super-set of the solution space is determined, in [18] a sufficient condition in terms of Hille-Yosida-type resolvent estimates is offered, in [14] with the help of integrated semigroups the well-posedness is investigated, and in [13], the solvability of a class of port-Hamiltonian DAEs is discussed.

As in [14], we will study the well-posedness of (1) using integrated semigroups. There is a strong connection between integrated semigroups and the growth rate of the resolvent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\lambda E-A)^{-1}$$\end{document}(λE-A)-1, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \rho (E,A)\ne \emptyset $$\end{document}λ∈ρ(E,A)≠∅, where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \rho (E,A):=\{\lambda \in \mathbb {C} \, \vert \, (\lambda E-A)^{-1} \in L(\mathcal {X},\mathcal {Z})\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ(E,A):={λ∈C|(λE-A)-1∈L(X,Z)}.A densely defined linear operator A generates an integrated semigroup if there exists an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N, such that3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\| (\lambda I -A)^{-1} \right\| \le C \left| \lambda \right| ^{n-1}, \quad \lambda \in \mathbb {C}_{\textrm{Re}\ge \omega }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(λI-A)-1≤Cλn-1,λ∈CRe≥ω,for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C, \omega >0$$\end{document}C,ω>0 [15, Thm. 4.8]. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=0$$\end{document}n=0 this matches the condition of a sectorial operator A, which is used for the generation of a analytic semigroup. Notice that (3) coincides with the complex resolvent index for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E=I$$\end{document}E=I. Based on this, we build on existing solution methods for the abstract Cauchy problem \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\hbox {d}}{{\hbox {d}}t}x(t)=Ax(t)$$\end{document}ddtx(t)=Ax(t), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x(0)=x_0$$\end{document}x(0)=x0 on the subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}(A^n) = \textrm{ran}\, ((\lambda I-A)^{-1})^n$$\end{document}D(An)=ran((λI-A)-1)n [10, Sect. 1.3] and extend these studies to the differential algebraic Eq. (1) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0 \in \textrm{ran}\,( (\lambda E-A)^{-1} E)^n$$\end{document}x0∈ran((λE-A)-1E)n. For further details on integrated semigroups we refer to [1, 15].

We subsequently focus on a special class of systems, namely, port-Hamiltonian differential algebraic equations (pH-DAEs). These systems provide a framework for modelling and analysing energy-dissipating physical systems, including electrical circuits, mechanical systems, fluid dynamic, thermal systems or robotics and control systems (just to name a few). There are different ways to define and approach pH-DAEs, such as using relations [4] or Dirac structures [24]. Here, we use an operator formulation, as in [13]. To be more specific, we consider4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\hbox {d}}{{\hbox {d}}t}Ex(t)=AQx(t),\quad t\ge 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtEx(t)=AQx(t),t≥0with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E,Q\in L(\mathcal {X},\mathcal {Z})$$\end{document}E,Q∈L(X,Z), Q invertible, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A:\mathcal {D}(A)\subseteq \mathcal {Z}\rightarrow \mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}A:D(A)⊆Z→Z closed, densely defined and dissipative and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E^*Q \ge 0$$\end{document}E∗Q≥0 non-negative, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle E^*Q x,x\rangle \ge 0$$\end{document}⟨E∗Qx,x⟩≥0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathcal {X}$$\end{document}x∈X. The Hamiltonian of the pH-DAE (4) is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle E^*Q x,x\rangle $$\end{document}⟨E∗Qx,x⟩. Additionally to this, we will assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{ran}\, E$$\end{document}ranE is closed. This guarantees the dissipation of the energy5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\hbox {d}}{{\hbox {d}}t}\langle E^*Q x(t), x(t)\rangle \le 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddt⟨E∗Qx(t),x(t)⟩≤0,along all classical solutions of (4).

The paper is organized as follows. In Sect. 2 a set of initial conditions for which (1) has a solution is characterized. In Sect. 3 the radiality index is defined and it is shown that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{ran}\, E$$\end{document}ranE is closed, existence of this index implies existence of a Weierstraß form. Several new results relating the radiality index and the degree of nilpotency are also obtained. Furthermore, provided that the complex resolvent index exists, and the Weierstraß form exists with densely defined \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1$$\end{document}A1, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1$$\end{document}A1 generates an integrated semigroup.

In Sect. 4 port-Hamiltonian DAEs are formally described and their dissipativity is proven. Solvability of port-Hamiltonian DAEs is studied in Sect. 5. With the aid of the results earlier in the paper, it is shown that pH-DAEs have complex resolvent index at most 3. This extends a result that is known in finite dimensions to the infinite-dimensional setting. Existence of solutions to pH-DAEs is then obtained, along with generation of an integrated semigroup.

Existence of solutions on a subspace

Throughout this article, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}Z be Hilbert spaces, and, if not mentioned otherwise, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A:\mathcal {D}(A)\subseteq \mathcal {X}\rightarrow \mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}A:D(A)⊆X→Z is a closed and densely defined operator and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\in L(\mathcal {X},\mathcal {Z})$$\end{document}E∈L(X,Z). In this section, we prove that if that the complex resolvent index exists, solutions exist on a subspace. We first recall the definition of the complex resolvent index, see [2, 22].

Theorem 2.2

Assume that (E, A) has a complex resolvent index. Then, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0 \in \textrm{ran}\, ((\mu E-A)^{-1} E)^{p_{\textrm{c,res}}^{(E,A)}+2}$$\end{document}x0∈ran((μE-A)-1E)pc,res(E,A)+2 there exists a unique classical solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x:\mathbb {R}_{\ge 0}\rightarrow \mathcal {X}$$\end{document}x:R≥0→X of (1).

Proof

Existence: Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p =p_{\textrm{c,res}}^{(E,A)}+2$$\end{document}p=pc,res(E,A)+2 and, for the sake of simplicity, define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(\mu ) :=((\mu E-A)^{-1} E)$$\end{document}R(μ):=((μE-A)-1E) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \in \rho (E,A)$$\end{document}μ∈ρ(E,A). By assumption, the complex resolvent index exists. Thus, there are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ,C >0$$\end{document}ω,C>0, such that7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\| R(\lambda ) \right\| \le C \left| \lambda \right| ^{p_{\textrm{c,res}}^{(E,A)}-1} \left\| E \right\| , \quad \lambda \in \mathbb {C}_{\textrm{Re} \ge \omega }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R(λ)≤Cλpc,res(E,A)-1E,λ∈CRe≥ω.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0 \in \textrm{ran}\, R(\mu )^p$$\end{document}x0∈ranR(μ)p. Thus, x is a classical solution of (1).

Remark 2.3

Of course it is also possible to formulate the initial condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x(0)=x_0$$\end{document}x(0)=x0 of (1) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \frac{d}{dt}Ex(t) = Ax(t),\quad t\ge 0,\\ Ex(0) = x_0. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtEx(t)=Ax(t),t≥0,Ex(0)=x0. Accordingly, one must assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0\in E(\textrm{ran}\, ((\mu E-A)^{-1} E)^{p_{\textrm{c,res}}^{(E,A)}+2})$$\end{document}x0∈E(ran((μE-A)-1E)pc,res(E,A)+2) in Theorem 2.2.The term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=p_{\textrm{c,res}}^{(E,A)}+2$$\end{document}p=pc,res(E,A)+2 in the proof of Theorem 2.2 is chosen in such a way that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{R(\lambda )}{(\lambda -\mu )^p}$$\end{document}R(λ)(λ-μ)p falls quickly enough, such that the integral in (9) not only exists; the function x(t) also becomes continuously differentiable. This means that the function x in (9) is only continuous, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0 \in \textrm{ran}\, ((\mu E-A)^{-1} E)^{p_{\textrm{c,res}}^{(E,A)}+1}$$\end{document}x0∈ran((μE-A)-1E)pc,res(E,A)+1. However, in this case x is a mild solution of (1), as one can compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Ex(t)- Ex(0)&= -\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int _{\omega -i \infty }^{\omega + i \infty } \underbrace{(\textrm{e}^{\lambda t }-1)}_{=\int _0^t \lambda \textrm{e}^{\lambda s}\,\textrm{d}s} \frac{E R(\lambda )z_0}{(\lambda -\mu )^p}\,\textrm{d}\lambda \\&= \int _0^t \bigg ( -\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int _{\omega -i \infty }^{\omega + i \infty } \lambda \textrm{e}^{\lambda s} \frac{{E}R(\lambda )z_0}{(\lambda -\mu )^p}\,\textrm{d}\lambda \bigg )\,\textrm{d}s\\&= \int _0^t \bigg ( -\frac{1}{2\pi i} \int _{\omega -i\infty }^{\omega +i\infty } \textrm{e}^{\lambda s} \frac{AR(\lambda )z_0}{(\lambda -\mu )^p}\,\textrm{d}\lambda - \underbrace{\frac{1}{2\pi i} \int _{\omega -i\infty }^{\omega +i\infty } \textrm{e}^{\lambda s} \frac{Ez_0}{(\lambda -\mu )^p}\,\textrm{d}\lambda }_{=0} \bigg ) \,\textrm{d}s \\&= \int _0^t Ax(s)\,\textrm{d}s = A \int _0^t x(s)\,\textrm{d}s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ex(t)-Ex(0)=-12πi∫ω-i∞ω+i∞(eλt-1)⏟=∫0tλeλsdsER(λ)z0(λ-μ)pdλ=∫0t(-12πi∫ω-i∞ω+i∞λeλsER(λ)z0(λ-μ)pdλ)ds=∫0t(-12πi∫ω-i∞ω+i∞eλsAR(λ)z0(λ-μ)pdλ-12πi∫ω-i∞ω+i∞eλsEz0(λ-μ)pdλ⏟=0)ds=∫0tAx(s)ds=A∫0tx(s)ds.In [22, Thm. 5.7] the set of initial values 14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} U:=\{x_0 \in \mathcal {X} \, \vert \, \exists x:\mathbb {R}_{\ge 0}\rightarrow \mathcal {X} \text { mild solution}\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}U:={x0∈X|∃x:R≥0→Xmild solution} was analysed in more detail. To be more precise, it was shown that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E^{-1} A$$\end{document}E-1A generates a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroup on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\textrm{ran}\, ((\mu E-A)^{-1} E)^{p_{\textrm{c,res}}^{(E,A)}+2}}$$\end{document}ran((μE-A)-1E)pc,res(E,A)+2¯ if and only if U is closed and for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0 \in U$$\end{document}x0∈U the mild solution is unique. The assumption that U is closed is a bit restrictive and it also requires information about U.In [5, Sect. 8] the existence of solutions on the subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\textrm{ran}\, R(\lambda )^k}$$\end{document}ranR(λ)k¯, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(\lambda ):=(\lambda E-A)^{-1} E$$\end{document}R(λ):=(λE-A)-1E, has been studied under slightly stronger conditions compared to the ones in Theorem 2.2. More specifically, under the assumption that there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge 1$$\end{document}k≥1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}ω∈R and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M>0$$\end{document}M>0, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\omega ,\infty )\subseteq \rho (E,A)$$\end{document}(ω,∞)⊆ρ(E,A) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\| R(\lambda )x\right\| \le \frac{M}{\lambda -\omega } \left\| x\right\| , \quad \forall \lambda >\omega , \; Then, under the assumption that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_R$$\end{document}AR generates a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroup, it was shown that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0\in \overline{\textrm{ran}\, R(\lambda )^k}$$\end{document}x0∈ranR(λ)k¯ there exists a unique mild solution of (1) and for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0 \in R(\lambda )\overline{\textrm{ran}\, R(\lambda )^k}$$\end{document}x0∈R(λ)ranR(λ)k¯ there exists a classical solution of (1). As it was remarked by the authors, the generation of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroup can be achieved if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\| \lambda R(\lambda ) x\right\| \le M \left\| x\right\| \quad \forall \lambda \in \mathbb {C}_{\ge \omega }, \; x\in \textrm{ran}\, R(\omega )^k \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λR(λ)x≤Mx∀λ∈C≥ω,x∈ranR(ω)k holds, which is a slightly stronger assumption compared to the complex resolvent index. Of course it is also possible to formulate the initial condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x(0)=x_0$$\end{document}x(0)=x0 of (1) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \frac{d}{dt}Ex(t) = Ax(t),\quad t\ge 0,\\ Ex(0) = x_0. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtEx(t)=Ax(t),t≥0,Ex(0)=x0. Of course it is also possible to formulate the initial condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x(0)=x_0$$\end{document}x(0)=x0 of (1) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \frac{d}{dt}Ex(t) = Ax(t),\quad t\ge 0,\\ Ex(0) = x_0. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtEx(t)=Ax(t),t≥0,Ex(0)=x0. The term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=p_{\textrm{c,res}}^{(E,A)}+2$$\end{document}p=pc,res(E,A)+2 in the proof of Theorem 2.2 is chosen in such a way that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{R(\lambda )}{(\lambda -\mu )^p}$$\end{document}R(λ)(λ-μ)p falls quickly enough, such that the integral in (9) not only exists; the function x(t) also becomes continuously differentiable. Then, under the assumption that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_R$$\end{document}AR generates a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroup, it was shown that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0\in \overline{\textrm{ran}\, R(\lambda )^k}$$\end{document}x0∈ranR(λ)k¯ there exists a unique mild solution of (1) and for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0 \in R(\lambda )\overline{\textrm{ran}\, R(\lambda )^k}$$\end{document}x0∈R(λ)ranR(λ)k¯ there exists a classical solution of (1). As it was remarked by the authors, the generation of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroup can be achieved if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\| \lambda R(\lambda ) x\right\| \le M \left\| x\right\| \quad \forall \lambda \in \mathbb {C}_{\ge \omega }, \; x\in \textrm{ran}\, R(\omega )^k \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λR(λ)x≤Mx∀λ∈C≥ω,x∈ranR(ω)k holds, which is a slightly stronger assumption compared to the complex resolvent index. In the latter case, we only have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\| \lambda R(\lambda ) R(\mu )^k x \right\| \le M \left\| x\right\| \quad \forall \lambda \in \mathbb {C}_{\ge \omega },\; x\in \mathcal {X}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λR(λ)R(μ)kx≤Mx∀λ∈C≥ω,x∈X, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=p_{\textrm{c,res}}^{(E,A)}$$\end{document}k=pc,res(E,A).

Sufficient condition for a Weierstraß form

In this section we start with the analysis of a sufficient condition for the existence of a Weierstraß form. To do that, we recall the definition of the radiality index [2] or, more generally, of the radiality [19].

We call two differential-algebraic equations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d}{dt}Ex=Ax$$\end{document}ddtEx=Ax and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d}{dt}{\tilde{E}} {\tilde{x}}={\tilde{A}}{\tilde{x}}$$\end{document}ddtE~x~=A~x~ defined on Hilbert spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}Z and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathcal {X}}$$\end{document}X~, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathcal {Z}}$$\end{document}Z~ equivalent, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(E,A)\sim ({\tilde{E}},{\tilde{A}})$$\end{document}(E,A)∼(E~,A~), if there are two bounded isomorphisms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P:\mathcal {X}\rightarrow \tilde{\mathcal {X}}$$\end{document}P:X→X~, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} Based on that, we define the notion of a Weierstraß form as follows.

Theorem 3.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\in L(\mathcal {X},\mathcal {Z})$$\end{document}E∈L(X,Z) with closed range and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A:\mathcal {D}(A)\subseteq \mathcal {X}\rightarrow \mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}A:D(A)⊆X→Z be a closed and densely defined operator. If (E, A) has a radiality index, then it also has a Weierstraß form, which is unique up to isomorphisms.

Remark 3.4

With (19c) one can rewrite the DAE \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d}{dt}Ex=Ax$$\end{document}ddtEx=Ax as follows20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\begin{bmatrix} I_{\mathcal {Z}^1} & 0 \\ 0 & E_2A_2^{-1}\end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix}z_1(t) \\ z_2(t)\end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} A_1E_1^{-1} & 0 \\ 0 & I_{\mathcal {Z}^2}\end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix}z_1(t) \\ z_2(t)\end{bmatrix}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtIZ100E2A2-1z1(t)z2(t)=A1E1-100IZ2z1(t)z2(t),on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}^1\times \mathcal {Z}^2$$\end{document}Z1×Z2.

Further, one can also show that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (E,A)\sim \left( \begin{bmatrix} I_{\mathcal {X}^1} & 0 \\ 0 & A_2^{-1} E_2 \end{bmatrix}, \begin{bmatrix} E_1^{-1} A_1 & 0 \\ 0 & I_{\mathcal {X}^2}\end{bmatrix}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(E,A)∼IX100A2-1E2,E1-1A100IX2and rewrite the DAE \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\frac{d}{dt}x = Ax$$\end{document}Eddtx=Ax in the following way21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{bmatrix} I_{\mathcal {X}^1} & 0 \\ 0 & A_2^{-1} E_2\end{bmatrix} \frac{d}{dt} \begin{bmatrix}x_1(t) \\ x_2(t)\end{bmatrix}&= \begin{bmatrix} E_1^{-1} A_1 & 0 \\ 0 & I_{\mathcal {X}^2}\end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix}x_1(t) \\ x_2(t)\end{bmatrix}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}IX100A2-1E2ddtx1(t)x2(t)=E1-1A100IX2x1(t)x2(t),on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}^1\times \mathcal {X}^2$$\end{document}X1×X2.

In the following, we will recall and generalize an example from [2, Ex. 6.5], which shows that the radiality index can be greater than 0.

Example 3.5

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W be a Hilbert space and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(A_0, \mathcal {D}(A_0))$$\end{document}(A0,D(A0)) generate a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroup on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b\in \mathcal {D}(A_0)$$\end{document}b∈D(A0) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c \in \mathcal {D}(A_0^*)$$\end{document}c∈D(A0∗) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle b, c\rangle \ne 0$$\end{document}⟨b,c⟩≠0 define the operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_u=bu$$\end{document}Bu=bu, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}u∈C and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Cz=\langle c,z\rangle $$\end{document}Cz=⟨c,z⟩ for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z\in \mathcal {W}$$\end{document}z∈W. We want to generalize the last statement a bit. To do this, we look again at the left- and right-E resolvents.

We want to show that the radiality index exists by using the Weierstraß form. Define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_b z = z-\frac{\langle c,z\rangle }{\langle c, b\rangle } b$$\end{document}Qbz=z-⟨c,z⟩⟨c,b⟩b. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Kz:={\tilde{C}} A_0 z = \frac{1}{\langle c,b\rangle } \langle c, A_0 z \rangle = \frac{1}{\langle c,b\rangle } \langle A_0^*c, z \rangle $$\end{document}Kz:=C~A0z=1⟨c,b⟩⟨c,A0z⟩=1⟨c,b⟩⟨A0∗c,z⟩ holds for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z\in W$$\end{document}z∈W and therefore K is a bounded operator on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W. Further examples of DAEs with existing radiality index (such as the linearized Navier–Stokes equation) can be found in [3]. Next, the radiality index for DAEs in Weierstraß form gets investigated.

Theorem 3.6

Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(E,A)= \left( \Big [{\begin{matrix} I_{\mathcal {Y}^1} & 0\\ 0 & N \end{matrix}}\Big ], \Big [{\begin{matrix} A_1 & 0\\ 0 & I_{\mathcal {Y}^2} \end{matrix}}\Big ]\right) $$\end{document}(E,A)=[IY100N],[A100IY2], where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1$$\end{document}A1 and N are defined as in (15). Then (E, A) has radiality index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{rad}}^{(E,A)}=p\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}prad(E,A)=p∈N if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(I_{\mathcal {Y}^1}, A_1)$$\end{document}(IY1,A1) has radiality index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{rad}}^{(E,A)}=p\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}prad(E,A)=p∈N and N has nilpotency degree smaller or equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p+1$$\end{document}p+1.If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1$$\end{document}A1 generates a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroup and N has nilpotency degree smaller or equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{rad}}^{(E,A)} = p$$\end{document}prad(E,A)=p.

(E, A) has radiality index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{rad}}^{(E,A)}=p\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}prad(E,A)=p∈N if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(I_{\mathcal {Y}^1}, A_1)$$\end{document}(IY1,A1) has radiality index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{rad}}^{(E,A)}=p\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}prad(E,A)=p∈N and N has nilpotency degree smaller or equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p+1$$\end{document}p+1.

(E, A) has radiality index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{rad}}^{(E,A)}=p\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}prad(E,A)=p∈N if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(I_{\mathcal {Y}^1}, A_1)$$\end{document}(IY1,A1) has radiality index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{rad}}^{(E,A)}=p\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}prad(E,A)=p∈N and N has nilpotency degree smaller or equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p+1$$\end{document}p+1.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1$$\end{document}A1 generates a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroup and N has nilpotency degree smaller or equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p+1$$\end{document}p+1, then (E, A) is p-radial and, especially, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{rad}}^{(E,A)} = p$$\end{document}prad(E,A)=p.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1$$\end{document}A1 generates a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroup and N has nilpotency degree smaller or equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p+1$$\end{document}p+1, then (E, A) is p-radial and, especially, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{rad}}^{(E,A)} = p$$\end{document}prad(E,A)=p.

Proof

Let N has nilpotency degree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k+1\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k+1∈N and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \rho (E,A)$$\end{document}λ∈ρ(E,A). Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \rho (I_{\mathcal {Y}^1}, A_1)$$\end{document}λ∈ρ(IY1,A1). Conversely, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \rho (I_{\mathcal {Y}^1}, A_1)$$\end{document}λ∈ρ(IY1,A1), then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\lambda N-I_{\mathcal {Y}^2})^{-1} = -\sum _{l=0}^k (\lambda N)^l$$\end{document}(λN-IY2)-1=-∑l=0k(λN)l. Thus,23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \rho (E,A)=\rho (I_{\mathcal {Y}^1},A_1) = \rho (A_1). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ(E,A)=ρ(IY1,A1)=ρ(A1).In particular, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \rho (E,A)$$\end{document}λ∈ρ(E,A) we obtain24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\lambda E- A)^{-1} E = \begin{bmatrix} (\lambda I_{\mathcal {Y}^1}-A_1)^{-1} & 0 \\ 0 & (\lambda N-I_{\mathcal {Y}^2})^{-1} N \end{bmatrix} = {E(\lambda E- A)^{-1}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(λE-A)-1E=(λIY1-A1)-100(λN-IY2)-1N=E(λE-A)-1and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \prod _{l=0}^k (\lambda _l E-A)^{-1} E=\Big [{\begin{matrix} \prod _{l=0}^k(\lambda _l I_{\mathcal {Y}^1}-A_1)^{-1} & 0\\ 0 & 0 \end{matrix}}\Big ] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∏l=0k(λlE-A)-1E=[∏l=0k(λlIY1-A1)-1000]for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _0,\ldots ,\lambda _p\in \rho ( E, A)$$\end{document}λ0,…,λp∈ρ(E,A). In particular, we have25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\| \prod _{l=0}^k(\lambda _l I_{\mathcal {Y}^1}-A_1)^{-1}\right\| = \left\| \prod _{l=0}^k (\lambda _l E-A)^{-1} E\right\| . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∏l=0k(λlIY1-A1)-1=∏l=0k(λlE-A)-1E.Thus, Part a) follows directly from (23) and (25). To show Part b) assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1$$\end{document}A1 generates a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroup and N has a nilpotency degree smaller or equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p+1$$\end{document}p+1. Using the theorem of Hille-Yosida there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega , C>0$$\end{document}ω,C>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\omega , \infty )\subseteq \rho (I_{\mathcal {Y}^1}, A_1) = \rho (A_1)$$\end{document}(ω,∞)⊆ρ(IY1,A1)=ρ(A1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert ((\lambda I_{\mathcal {Y}^1}-A_1)^{-1})^n\Vert \le \frac{C}{(\lambda -\omega )^n}$$\end{document}‖((λIY1-A1)-1)n‖≤C(λ-ω)n for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \ge \omega $$\end{document}λ≥ω, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N. Together with (24) and (25) we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\| \left( \prod _{l=0}^p (\lambda _l E-A)^{-1} E\right) ^n\right\| = \left\| \left( \prod _{l=0}^p E(\lambda _l E- A)^{-1} \right) ^n\right\| \le \frac{C^{p+1}}{\left( \prod _{l=0}^{p+1}\lambda _l-\omega \right) ^n} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∏l=0p(λlE-A)-1En=∏l=0pE(λlE-A)-1n≤Cp+1∏l=0p+1λl-ωnfor all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _0,\ldots ,\lambda _{p+1}\ge \omega $$\end{document}λ0,…,λp+1≥ω. Thus, (E, A) is p-radial. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

A direct consequence of Theorem 3.6 is the equality of different index terms introduced in [2]. Here, we will denote the differentiation index, the chain index and the perturbation index by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{diff}}^{(E,A)}$$\end{document}pdiff(E,A), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{chain}}^{(E,A)}$$\end{document}pchain(E,A) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{pert}}^{(E,A)}$$\end{document}ppert(E,A).

Corollary 3.7

Assume that (E, A) has a Weierstraß form and nilpotency index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{nilp}}^{(E,A)}$$\end{document}pnilp(E,A), i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(E,A)\sim \left( \Big [{\begin{matrix} I_{\mathcal {Y}^1} & 0\\ 0 & N \end{matrix}}\Big ], \Big [{\begin{matrix} A_1 & 0\\ 0 & I_{\mathcal {Y}^2} \end{matrix}}\Big ]\right) $$\end{document}(E,A)∼[IY100N],[A100IY2]. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1$$\end{document}A1 generates a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroup on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Y}^1$$\end{document}Y1, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_{\textrm{rad}}^{(E,A)}+1 = p_{\mathrm{res\phantom {d}}}^{(E,A)} = p_{\textrm{nilp}}^{(E,A)} = p_{\textrm{diff}}^{(E,A)} = p_{\textrm{chain}}^{(E,A)} = p_{\textrm{pert}}^{(E,A)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}prad(E,A)+1=presd(E,A)=pnilp(E,A)=pdiff(E,A)=pchain(E,A)=ppert(E,A).

Proof

This follows from Theorem 3.6 and [2]. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Next, we want to investigate the connection between integrated semigroups and linear differential algebraic equations.

With this information we can illustrate the relation between the complex resolvent of a DAE and the generator of an integrated semigroup, given that (E, A) has a Weierstraß form.

Theorem 3.9

Assume that (E, A) has a Weierstraß form as in (15). If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1$$\end{document}A1 is densely defined and (E, A) has a complex resolvent index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{c, res}}^{(E,A)}$$\end{document}pc, res(E,A), then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1$$\end{document}A1 generates an (at least) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{c, res}}^{(E,A)}+2$$\end{document}pc, res(E,A)+2-times integrated semigroup.

Proof

Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(E,A)\sim \left( \Big [{\begin{matrix} I_{\mathcal {Y}^1} & 0\\ 0 & N \end{matrix}}\Big ], \Big [{\begin{matrix} A_1 & 0\\ 0 & I_{\mathcal {Y}^2} \end{matrix}}\Big ]\right) $$\end{document}(E,A)∼[IY100N],[A100IY2] has a complex resolvent index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=p_{\textrm{c,res}}^{(E,A)}$$\end{document}p=pc,res(E,A). Then, by (23) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho (E,A)=\rho (A_1)$$\end{document}ρ(E,A)=ρ(A1) and27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert (\lambda I_{\mathcal {Y}^1}-A_1)^{-1} \Vert \le {\tilde{C}} \Vert (\lambda E-A)^{-1} \Vert \le C \Vert \lambda \Vert ^{p-1}, \quad \lambda \ge \omega , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖(λIY1-A1)-1‖≤C~‖(λE-A)-1‖≤C‖λ‖p-1,λ≥ω,for given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C, {\tilde{C}}>0$$\end{document}C,C~>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega >0$$\end{document}ω>0. Thus, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(I_{\mathcal {Y}^1},A_1)$$\end{document}(IY1,A1) has a complex resolvent index and by [15, Cor. 4.9] \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1$$\end{document}A1 generates an (at least) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p+2$$\end{document}p+2-times integrated semigroup. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Corollary 3.10

Assume that (E, A) has a complex radiality index. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1E_1^{-1}$$\end{document}A1E1-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1^{-1} A_1$$\end{document}E1-1A1 from (20) and (21) generate integrated semigroups.

Proof

In [2, Prop. 5.4] it was shown that the existence of the radiality index already implies the existence of the resolvent index. This implication extends naturally to the complex resolvent and complex radiality index. Therefore, if (E, A) has a complex radiality index, then by Theorem 3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(E,A)\sim \left( \Big [{\begin{matrix} I_{\mathcal {Z}^1} & 0\\ 0 & E_2A_2^{-1} \end{matrix}}\Big ], \Big [ {\begin{matrix} A_1E_1^{-1} & 0\\ 0 & I_{\mathcal {Z}^2} \end{matrix}}\Big ]\right) \sim \left( \Big [{\begin{matrix} I_{\mathcal {X}^1} & 0\\ 0 & A_2^{-1} E_2 \end{matrix}}\Big ], \Big [ {\begin{matrix} E_1^{-1} A_1 & 0\\ 0 & I_{\mathcal {X}^2} \end{matrix}}\Big ]\right) $$\end{document}(E,A)∼[IZ100E2A2-1],[A1E1-100IZ2]∼[IX100A2-1E2],[E1-1A100IX2] and by Theorem 3.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1E_1^{-1}$$\end{document}A1E1-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1^{-1} A_1$$\end{document}E1-1A1 generate integrated semigroups. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Infinite-dimensional port-Hamiltonian DAEs

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}, \mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}X,Z be Hilbert spaces, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E, Q\in L(\mathcal {X},\mathcal {Z})$$\end{document}E,Q∈L(X,Z) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A:\mathcal {D}(A)\subseteq \mathcal {Z}\rightarrow \mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}A:D(A)⊆Z→Z be a closed and densely defined operator. Then, the following differential-algebraic equation28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \frac{d}{d t} Ex(t) =AQx(t), \quad t\ge 0,\\ Ex(0) =z_0, \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtEx(t)=AQx(t),t≥0,Ex(0)=z0,for a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0\in \mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}z0∈Z is called a port-Hamiltonian differential-algebraic equation (short pH-DAE), if A is dissipative, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{ran}\, E$$\end{document}ranE is closed, Q is invertible and29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E^*Q = Q^*E\ge 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E∗Q=Q∗E≥0holds. In this context, we call30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H(x):=\langle Ex,Qx\rangle _\mathcal {Z},\quad x\in \mathcal {X} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H(x):=⟨Ex,Qx⟩Z,x∈Xthe Hamiltonian of (E, AQ). Note that here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T:\mathcal {X}\rightarrow \mathcal {X}$$\end{document}T:X→X is called non-negative, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\ge 0$$\end{document}T≥0, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle Tx,x\rangle _\mathcal {X}\ge 0$$\end{document}⟨Tx,x⟩X≥0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathcal {X}$$\end{document}x∈X.

These systems provide a framework for modelling and analysing energy-dissipating physical systems, including electrical circuits, mechanical systems, fluid dynamic, thermal systems or robotics and control systems. In this section we want to examine a few properties of pH-DAEs and show that the derivative of the Hamiltonian dissipates on all classical solutions of (28).

Remark 4.1
