Thus, the representation for port-Hamiltonian differential algebraic equations chosen here corresponds with the notion of an abstract Hamiltonian differential-algebraic equation defined in [13, Assumption 7]. Furthermore, it should be mentioned that there are more ways to define and approach port-Hamiltonian systems, such as using relations [4] or Dirac structures [24].

Next, we want to show that the derivative of the Hamiltonian dissipates on solutions, i.e. for a solution of (28), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d}{dt}H(x(t))\le 0$$\end{document}ddtH(x(t))≤0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\ge 0$$\end{document}t≥0.

Proposition 4.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B\in L(\mathcal {Z})$$\end{document}B∈L(Z) be a non-negative and self-adjoint operator on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}Z and assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{ran}\, B$$\end{document}ranB is closed. Then there exists a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c>0$$\end{document}c>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\in L(\mathcal {Z})$$\end{document}T∈L(Z) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\ge cI_\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}T≥cIZ and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} BTB=B. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}BTB=B.Furthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdot , \cdot \rangle _T:=\langle T \cdot , \cdot \rangle $$\end{document}⟨·,·⟩T:=⟨T·,·⟩ induces a norm on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}Z equivalent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}‖·‖Z.

Obviously, if B is invertible, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T=B^{-1}$$\end{document}T=B-1.

Proof

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{ran}\, B$$\end{document}ranB is closed and B is self-adjoint, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}Z has an orthogonal decomposition into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}=\textrm{ran}\, B\,\oplus \,\textrm{ker}\, B$$\end{document}Z=ranB⊕kerB. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{\textrm{ran}\, B}$$\end{document}PranB and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{\textrm{ker}\, B}$$\end{document}PkerB be the projections of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}Z onto \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{ran}\, B$$\end{document}ranB and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{ker}\, B$$\end{document}kerB. Define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{B}} :\textrm{ran}\, B \rightarrow \textrm{ran}\, B$$\end{document}B~:ranB→ranB, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{B}}(z) = Bz$$\end{document}B~(z)=Bz. This is a bounded, positive self-adjoint operator on the Hilbert space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{ran}\, B$$\end{document}ranB, which is bijective (note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{ker}\, {\tilde{B}} = \textrm{ker}\, B \cap \textrm{ran}\, B = \{0\}$$\end{document}kerB~=kerB∩ranB={0}). Hence, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{B}}^{-1}$$\end{document}B~-1 is bounded, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{B}}$$\end{document}B~ is strictly positive with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle {\tilde{B}} z_1, z_1 \rangle \ge c \Vert z_1\Vert ^2, \quad z_1\in \textrm{ran}\, B \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨B~z1,z1⟩≥c‖z1‖2,z1∈ranBfor all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_1 \in \textrm{ran}\, B$$\end{document}z1∈ranB, for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c>0$$\end{document}c>0.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota _{\textrm{ran}\, B}:\textrm{ran}\, B\rightarrow \mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}ιranB:ranB→Z be the embedding and define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T:\iota _{\textrm{ran}\, B}{\tilde{B}}^{-1} \iota _{\textrm{ran}\, B}^*+ P_{\textrm{ker}\, B}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T:ιranBB~-1ιranB∗+PkerB.This operator satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$BTB=B$$\end{document}BTB=B and is bounded. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z= z_1+ z_2 \in \mathcal {Z}=\textrm{ran}\, B \, \oplus \textrm{ker}\, B$$\end{document}z=z1+z2∈Z=ranB⊕kerB. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle Tz, z \rangle&= \langle {\tilde{B}} z_1 + z_2 , z_1 + z_2 \rangle \\&= \langle {\tilde{B}} z_1 , z_1 \rangle + \langle z_2 ,z_2 \rangle \\&\ge c \Vert z_1\Vert ^2 + \Vert z_2\Vert ^2 . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨Tz,z⟩=⟨B~z1+z2,z1+z2⟩=⟨B~z1,z1⟩+⟨z2,z2⟩≥c‖z1‖2+‖z2‖2.Thus, T is strictly positive. The self-adjointness of T follows from that of B, completing the proof. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Corollary 4.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E,Q\in L(\mathcal {X},\mathcal {Z})$$\end{document}E,Q∈L(X,Z), whereby E has a closed range, Q is invertible and (29) holds. Then, there exists a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c>0$$\end{document}c>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\in L(\mathcal {Z})$$\end{document}T∈L(Z) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\ge cI_\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}T≥cIZ and33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E^*TE = E^*Q = Q^*E. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E∗TE=E∗Q=Q∗E.Furthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdot , \cdot \rangle _T :=\langle T\cdot , \cdot \rangle $$\end{document}⟨·,·⟩T:=⟨T·,·⟩ induces a norm on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}Z equivalent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}‖·‖Z.

Proof

This follows from Remark 4.1, Proposition 4.2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B=EQ^{-1}$$\end{document}B=EQ-1 and the fact that Q is invertible. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Theorem 4.4

Let (E, AQ) be a pH-DAE with Hamiltonian H. Then, for all classical solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x:\mathbb {R}_{\ge 0}\rightarrow \mathcal {X}$$\end{document}x:R≥0→X\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\langle E x(t), Qx(t)\rangle \le 0, \quad t\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddt⟨Ex(t),Qx(t)⟩≤0,t≥0.

Proof

By Corollary 4.3 we know that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\ge cI_Z$$\end{document}T≥cIZ, such that (33) holds. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x:\mathbb {R}_{\ge 0}\rightarrow \mathcal {X}$$\end{document}x:R≥0→X be a classical solution of (28). Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\langle Ex(t), Qx(t) \rangle _\mathcal {Z}&= \frac{d}{dt} \langle x(t), \underbrace{E^*Q}_{=E^*T E} x(t)\rangle _\mathcal {Z} \\&= 2\textrm{Re} \left\langle Ex(t), \frac{d}{dt} TEx(t)\right\rangle _\mathcal {Z} \\&= 2\textrm{Re} \left\langle Qx(t), \frac{d}{dt}Ex(t)\right\rangle _\mathcal {Z}\\&= 2\textrm{Re} \left\langle Qx(t), AQx(t)\right\rangle _\mathcal {Z} \le 0, \quad t\ge 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddt⟨Ex(t),Qx(t)⟩Z=ddt⟨x(t),E∗Q⏟=E∗TEx(t)⟩Z=2ReEx(t),ddtTEx(t)Z=2ReQx(t),ddtEx(t)Z=2ReQx(t),AQx(t)Z≤0,t≥0,where we used the continuity of E, Q and T in the second and third equation. Note that we used \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle Ex(t) = TEx(t)\rangle = \langle x(t), E^*T E x(t) \rangle = \langle x(t), Q^*Ex(t) \rangle = \langle Qx(t), Ex(t)\rangle $$\end{document}⟨Ex(t)=TEx(t)⟩=⟨x(t),E∗TEx(t)⟩=⟨x(t),Q∗Ex(t)⟩=⟨Qx(t),Ex(t)⟩ in the third equality. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 4.5

From the proof of Theorem 4.4 it becomes clear that for all classical solutions x of (28) the DAE is already dissipating, i.e. one has\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Re} \langle E x(t), AQx(t)\rangle _T \le 0, \quad t\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Re⟨Ex(t),AQx(t)⟩T≤0,t≥0.It is possible to show the same for the adjoint system and, if the set containing all trajectories of the solutions of (28) is closed, one can apply [7, Thm. 3.7] to gain a Weierstraß form on a subset. Thus, there also exists a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\in L(\mathcal {X})$$\end{document}S∈L(X) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} {X}$$\end{document}S≥c~IX, for a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{c}}>0$$\end{document}c~>0, with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} ESE^*= EQ^{-1} = Q^{-*} E^*\end{aligned}$$\end{document}ESE∗=EQ-1=Q-∗E∗and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} Assuming that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^*$$\end{document}A∗ is dissipative, one has\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Re}\langle (E)^{*}_{S^{-1}, T} z(t), (AQ)^{*}_{S^{-1}, T} z(T)\rangle _{S ^{-1}}&= \textrm{Re}\langle E^*T z(t), SQ^*A^*Tz(T)\rangle _\mathcal {X} \\&= \textrm{Re}\langle E^*T z(t), \frac{d}{dt} S E^*T z(T)\rangle _\mathcal {X}\\&= \textrm{Re}\langle T z(t), \frac{d}{dt} E S E^*T z(T)\rangle _\mathcal {X}\\&= \textrm{Re}\langle Q^{-1} T z(t), S^{-1} \frac{d}{dt} S E^*T z(T)\rangle _\mathcal {X}\\&= \textrm{Re}\langle Q^{-1} T z(t), S^{-1} S Q^*A^*T z(T)\rangle _\mathcal {X}\\&= \textrm{Re}\langle T z(t), A^*T z(T)\rangle _\mathcal {X} \le 0, \quad t\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Re⟨(E)S-1,T∗z(t),(AQ)S-1,T∗z(T)⟩S-1=Re⟨E∗Tz(t),SQ∗A∗Tz(T)⟩X=Re⟨E∗Tz(t),ddtSE∗Tz(T)⟩X=Re⟨Tz(t),ddtESE∗Tz(T)⟩X=Re⟨Q-1Tz(t),S-1ddtSE∗Tz(T)⟩X=Re⟨Q-1Tz(t),S-1SQ∗A∗Tz(T)⟩X=Re⟨Tz(t),A∗Tz(T)⟩X≤0,t≥0.Thus, Example 4.6

Consider longitudinal vibrations in a viscoelastic nanorod. Since the various physical constants in Q are positive, it is easy to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E^*Q$$\end{document}E∗Q is non-negative and self-adjoint and with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle Az,z\rangle _\mathcal {X}&= \int _0^l z_2(x)\frac{\partial z_5(x)}{\partial x} + z_5(x)\frac{\partial z_2(x)}{\partial x} - b^2 z_2^2(x) - (CD\tau _d + \mu b^2)z^2_3(x)\,\text {d}x\\ &= -b^2 \left\| z_2\right\| _\mathcal {X}^2 - (CD\tau _d + \mu b^2)\left\| z_3\right\| _\mathcal {X}^2 \le 0, \quad z=(z_1,\ldots ,z_5)\in \mathcal {D}(A), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨Az,z⟩X=∫0lz2(x)∂z5(x)∂x+z5(x)∂z2(x)∂x-b2z22(x)-(CDτd+μb2)z32(x)dx=-b2z2X2-(CDτd+μb2)z3X2≤0,z=(z1,…,z5)∈D(A),A is dissipative. Now, as in [6, p. 452] we impose the boundary conditions directly on the space and show that (38) has radiality index 0. By [7, Thm. 3.7] it becomes clear that if (E, AQ) is a pH-DAE with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Re} \langle AQx, Ex\rangle \le 0$$\end{document}Re⟨AQx,Ex⟩≤0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Re} \langle (AQ)^*z, E^*z\rangle \le 0$$\end{document}Re⟨(AQ)∗z,E∗z⟩≤0, then (E, AQ) is in fact 0-radial (and therefore has radiality index 0), admits a Weierstraß form as in (20) or (21) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(AQ)_1E_1^{-1}$$\end{document}(AQ)1E1-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1^{-1} (AQ)_1$$\end{document}E1-1(AQ)1 generate contraction semigroups on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathcal {Z}}^1$$\end{document}Z~1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathcal {X}}^1 $$\end{document}X~1. Weierstraß form and solutions of port-Hamiltonian DAEs

It is possible to show that the (complex) resolvent index for pH-DAEs always exists and that it is bounded by 2 (3). This is an already widely known result in finite dimensions and was shown for the infinite-dimensional case in [2] for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}=\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}X=Z and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q=I$$\end{document}Q=I.

Proposition 5.1

Let E, Q and A be defined as before, such that (E, AQ) defines a pH-DAE. Assume that there exists a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega >0$$\end{document}ω>0 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\omega ,\infty )\subseteq \rho (E,AQ)$$\end{document}(ω,∞)⊆ρ(E,AQ) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathbb {C}_{\textrm{Re} >\omega }\subseteq \rho (E,AQ))$$\end{document}(CRe>ω⊆ρ(E,AQ)). Then the (complex) resolvent index exists and is at most 2 (at most 3).

Proof

Using Remark 4.1 and [2, Thm. 3.3] one knows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(EQ^{-1}, A)$$\end{document}(EQ-1,A) is a pH-DAE with an existing (complex) resolvent index, which is at most 2 (at most 3), and together with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\lambda EQ^{-1} -A)^{-1}=Q(\lambda E-AQ)^{-1}$$\end{document}(λEQ-1-A)-1=Q(λE-AQ)-1 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \rho (EQ^{-1}, A) = \rho (E,AQ)$$\end{document}λ∈ρ(EQ-1,A)=ρ(E,AQ) the assertion follows. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Proposition 5.2

Let E, Q and A be defined as before, such that (E, AQ) defines a pH-DAE and assume that there exists a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega >0$$\end{document}ω>0 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}_{\textrm{Re} >\omega }\subseteq \rho (E,AQ)$$\end{document}CRe>ω⊆ρ(E,AQ). Then, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0 \in \textrm{ran}\,((\mu E-AQ)^{-1} E)^{p_{\textrm{c,res}}^{(E,AQ)}+2}$$\end{document}x0∈ran((μE-AQ)-1E)pc,res(E,AQ)+2 there exists a unique solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x:\mathbb {R}_{\ge 0}\rightarrow \mathcal {X}$$\end{document}x:R≥0→X of the following differential algebraic equation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \frac{d}{dt}Ex(t) = AQx(t), \quad t\ge 0,\\ x(0) =x_0. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtEx(t)=AQx(t),t≥0,x(0)=x0.

Proof

This is a direct consequence of Theorem 2.2 and Proposition 5.1. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The next goal is to examine the well-posedness of a pH-DAE on the whole domain, given that the radiality index exists. Let (E, AQ) be such a pH-DAE. By Theorem 3.3 (28) is equivalent to41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\begin{bmatrix} I_{\mathcal {Z}^1}^{-1} & 0\\ 0 & E_2(AQ)_2^{-1} \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} z_1(t) \\ z_2(t) \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} (AQ)_1E_1^{-1} & 0 \\ 0 & I_{\mathcal {Z}^2} \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} z_1(t) \\ z_2(t) \end{bmatrix}, \quad t\ge 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtIZ1-100E2(AQ)2-1z1(t)z2(t)=(AQ)1E1-100IZ2z1(t)z2(t),t≥0and (31) is equivalent to42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{bmatrix} I_{\mathcal {X}^1}^{-1} & 0\\ 0 & (AQ)_2^{-1} E_2 \end{bmatrix} \frac{d}{dt} \begin{bmatrix} x_1(t) \\ x_2(t) \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} E_1^{-1}(AQ)_1 & 0 \\ 0 & I_{\mathcal {X}^2} \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x_1(t) \\ x_2(t) \end{bmatrix}, \quad t\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}IX1-100(AQ)2-1E2ddtx1(t)x2(t)=E1-1(AQ)100IX2x1(t)x2(t),t≥0.The main problem with a transformation like that is that (41) and (42) are not necessarily pH-DAEs anymore. Thus, the resulting question is under which circumstances \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(AQ)_1E_1^{-1}$$\end{document}(AQ)1E1-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1^{-1}(AQ)_1$$\end{document}E1-1(AQ)1 generate a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroup. A possible condition for that is AQ being strongly (E, p)-radial [19, Thm. 2.6.1].

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathcal {X}$$\end{document}x∈X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \rho (E,AQ)$$\end{document}λ∈ρ(E,AQ) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda >0$$\end{document}λ>0. Thus, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\lambda E-AQ)^{-1} \in L(\mathcal {Z}, \mathcal {X})$$\end{document}(λE-AQ)-1∈L(Z,X). As seen in the proof of Theorem 3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(AQ)_1E_1^{-1}$$\end{document}(AQ)1E1-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1^{-1} (AQ)_1$$\end{document}E1-1(AQ)1 are densely defined and by Proposition 5.1 the complex resolvent index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{c,res}}^{(E,AQ)}$$\end{document}pc,res(E,AQ) exists. Thus, by Theorem 3.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(I_{\mathcal {Z}^1}, (AQ)_1E_1^{-1})$$\end{document}(IZ1,(AQ)1E1-1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(I_{\mathcal {X}^1}, E_1^{-1} (AQ)_1)$$\end{document}(IX1,E1-1(AQ)1) generate an (at least) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\textrm{c, res}}^{(E,AQ)}+2$$\end{document}pc, res(E,AQ)+2-times integrated semigroup.

In order to show a), assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^*(\mathcal {Z}^1) = Q^*(E(\mathcal {X}^1))= \mathcal {X}^1$$\end{document}Q∗(Z1)=Q∗(E(X1))=X1 holds. Thus, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^*E:\mathcal {X}^1\rightarrow \mathcal {X}^1$$\end{document}Q∗E:X1→X1 is non-negative and self-adjoint. In fact, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^*E$$\end{document}Q∗E is self-adjoint the mappings \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^*E \pm {\textrm{i}} I_\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}Q∗E±iIX are surjective. Hence, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z\in \mathcal {X}^1$$\end{document}z∈X1 there are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_\pm = x_\pm ^1+x_\pm ^2 \in \mathcal {X}=\mathcal {X}^1\oplus \mathcal {X}^2$$\end{document}x±=x±1+x±2∈X=X1⊕X2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(Q^*E\pm {\textrm{i}} I_\mathcal {X})x_\pm = z$$\end{document}(Q∗E±iIX)x±=z. Together with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^*E(\mathcal {X}^1)=\mathcal {X}^1$$\end{document}Q∗E(X1)=X1 one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PQ^*E x_\pm = PQ^*E x_\pm ^1 + PQ^*Ex_\pm ^2 = Q^*E Px_\pm ^1 = Q^*E Px_\pm $$\end{document}PQ∗Ex±=PQ∗Ex±1+PQ∗Ex±2=Q∗EPx±1=Q∗EPx± and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} z&= Pz\\&= PQ^*E x_\pm \pm {\textrm{i}} Px_\pm \\&= Q^*E Px_\pm \pm {\textrm{i}} Px_\pm = (Q^*E \pm {\textrm{i}} I_{\mathcal {X}^1}) x_\pm . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}z=Pz=PQ∗Ex±±iPx±=Q∗EPx±±iPx±=(Q∗E±iIX1)x±.Thus, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^*E \pm {\textrm{i}} I_{\mathcal {X}^1}:\mathcal {X}^1\rightarrow \mathcal {X}^1$$\end{document}Q∗E±iIX1:X1→X1 are surjective and consequently, as a closed operator, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(Q^*E)\vert _{\mathcal {X}^1}$$\end{document}(Q∗E)|X1 is self-adjoint. Furthermore, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^*$$\end{document}Q∗ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1$$\end{document}E1 are invertible and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(Q^*E)\vert _{\mathcal {X}^1}$$\end{document}(Q∗E)|X1 is self-adjoint, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(Q^*E)\vert _{\mathcal {X}^1}$$\end{document}(Q∗E)|X1 becomes invertible and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((Q^*E)\vert _{\mathcal {X}^1})^{-1}$$\end{document}((Q∗E)|X1)-1 self-adjoint as well. Hence, using Lemma 4.2 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B=((Q^*E)\vert _{\mathcal {X}^1})^{-1}$$\end{document}B=((Q∗E)|X1)-1 there exists a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c>0$$\end{document}c>0, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T=((Q^*E)\vert _{\mathcal {X}^1})\ge cI_{\mathcal {X}^1}$$\end{document}T=((Q∗E)|X1)≥cIX1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdot , \cdot \rangle _{\tilde{\mathcal {X}}^1}:=\langle (Q^*E)\vert _{\mathcal {X}^1} \cdot ,\cdot \rangle $$\end{document}⟨·,·⟩X~1:=⟨(Q∗E)|X1·,·⟩ induces a norm equivalent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}‖·‖X on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}^1$$\end{document}X1. Define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathcal {X}}^1:=(\mathcal {X}^1, \langle \cdot , \cdot \rangle _{\tilde{\mathcal {X}}^1})$$\end{document}X~1:=(X1,⟨·,·⟩X~1). Since A is dissipative one derives\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Re} \langle E_1^{-1}(AQ)_1 x, x\rangle _{\tilde{\mathcal {X}}^1} = \textrm{Re} \langle AQx, Qx\rangle _\mathcal {X} \le 0, \quad x\in \mathcal {D}(E_1^{-1} (AQ)_1), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Re⟨E1-1(AQ)1x,x⟩X~1=Re⟨AQx,Qx⟩X≤0,x∈D(E1-1(AQ)1),which means that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1^{-1}(AQ)_1$$\end{document}E1-1(AQ)1 is dissipative in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathcal {X}}^1$$\end{document}X~1. Given that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}=\mathcal {X}^1\oplus \mathcal {X}^2$$\end{document}X=X1⊕X2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1$$\end{document}E1 is bijective and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\lambda E_1 -(AQ)_1)$$\end{document}(λE1-(AQ)1) is surjective, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\lambda I_{\mathcal {X}^1}-E_1^{-1}(AQ)_1) = E_1^{-1}(\lambda E_1-(AQ)_1):\mathcal {D}((AQ)_1)\subseteq \mathcal {X}^1\rightarrow \mathcal {X}^1$$\end{document}(λIX1-E1-1(AQ)1)=E1-1(λE1-(AQ)1):D((AQ)1)⊆X1→X1 becomes surjective as well.

By the theorem of Lumer-Phillips [1, Thm.3.4.5] \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1^{-1}(AQ)_1$$\end{document}E1-1(AQ)1 generates a contraction semigroup on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathcal {X}}^1$$\end{document}X~1. In this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1^{-1}(AQ)_1$$\end{document}E1-1(AQ)1 also generates a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroup on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}^1$$\end{document}X1.

To show b) assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q(\mathcal {X}^1)=Q(E^{-1} (\mathcal {Z}^1))=\mathcal {Z}^1$$\end{document}Q(X1)=Q(E-1(Z1))=Z1 holds. As seen before it is possible to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$QE^{-1}$$\end{document}QE-1 is non-negative, self-adjoint and has a non-negative and self-adjoint inverse, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$QE^{-1}\ge cI_{\mathcal {X}}$$\end{document}QE-1≥cIX for a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c>0$$\end{document}c>0. Define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdot ,\cdot \rangle _{\tilde{\mathcal {Z}}^1}:=\langle QE^{-1} \cdot , \cdot \rangle $$\end{document}⟨·,·⟩Z~1:=⟨QE-1·,·⟩ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathcal {Z}}^1 :=(\mathcal {Z}^1, \langle \cdot ,\cdot \rangle _{\tilde{\mathcal {Z}}^1})$$\end{document}Z~1:=(Z1,⟨·,·⟩Z~1). Thus,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Re} \langle (AQ)_1E_1^{-1} z, z \rangle _{\tilde{\mathcal {Z}}^1} = \textrm{Re}\langle A QE_1 z, QE_1 z\rangle \le 0,\quad z\in \mathcal {D}((AQ)_1E_1^{-1}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Re⟨(AQ)1E1-1z,z⟩Z~1=Re⟨AQE1z,QE1z⟩≤0,z∈D((AQ)1E1-1)since A is dissipative. Then, the rest of this proof follows the previous part. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 5.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}=\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}X=Z and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q=I_\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}Q=IX and assume that E commutes with A on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}(A)$$\end{document}D(A). Thus\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Ex=E(\lambda E-A)(\lambda E-A)^{-1} x = (\lambda E-A)E (\lambda E-A)^{-1} x \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ex=E(λE-A)(λE-A)-1x=(λE-A)E(λE-A)-1xor equivalently \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\lambda E-A)^{-1} Ex=E(\lambda E-A)^{-1} x$$\end{document}(λE-A)-1Ex=E(λE-A)-1x. Thus, the left- and right-E resolvent coincide and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}^1=\mathcal {Z}^1$$\end{document}X1=Z1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}^2=\mathcal {Z}^2$$\end{document}X2=Z2 as well as the projections from Theorem 3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P=R$$\end{document}P=R. In this case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^*E(\mathcal {X}^1)=\mathcal {X}^1$$\end{document}Q∗E(X1)=X1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$QE^{-1}(\mathcal {Z}^1)=\mathcal {Z}^1$$\end{document}QE-1(Z1)=Z1 hold. Unfortunately, the assumption that E and AQ commute massively limits the choice of systems. Because if one considers the following type of system\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt} \underbrace{\begin{bmatrix} I & 0\\ 0 & N \end{bmatrix}}_{=:E} \begin{bmatrix} x_1(t) \\ x_2(t) \end{bmatrix} = \underbrace{\begin{bmatrix} A_1 & A_2 \\ A_3 & A_4 \end{bmatrix}}_{=:A} \begin{bmatrix} x_1(t) \\ x_2(t) \end{bmatrix}, \quad t\ge 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtI00N⏟=:Ex1(t)x2(t)=A1A2A3A4⏟=:Ax1(t)x2(t),t≥0,and additionally assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$EA=AE$$\end{document}EA=AE hold, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_2=A_2N$$\end{document}A2=A2N, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$NA_3=A_3$$\end{document}NA3=A3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$NA_4=A_4N$$\end{document}NA4=A4N.

Example 5.5

Consider the system\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix} I & 0\\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}}_{=\mathcal {E}} \begin{bmatrix} x_1(t)\\ x_2(t) \end{bmatrix} = \underbrace{\begin{bmatrix} A_1 & A_2\\ A_3 & A_4 \end{bmatrix}}_{=\mathcal {A}} \begin{bmatrix} x_1(t)\\ x_2(t) \end{bmatrix} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtI000⏟=Ex1(t)x2(t)=A1A2A3A4⏟=Ax1(t)x2(t)with existing radiality index and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}$$\end{document}A being dissipative.

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{ker}\, E\subseteq \mathcal {X}^2$$\end{document}kerE⊆X2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}=\mathcal {X}^1\oplus \mathcal {X}^2$$\end{document}X=X1⊕X2, one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_1=I_{\mathcal {X}^1}$$\end{document}E1=IX1. Thus, one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}(\mathcal {X}^1)= \mathcal {X}^1 = \mathcal {Z}^1 = \mathcal {E}_1(\mathcal {Z}^1)$$\end{document}E(X1)=X1=Z1=E1(Z1). But, in this case, it is even possible to say directly something about the generation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroups without using Theorem 5.3. Because in such a case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}_1 = \mathcal {A}_1\mathcal {E}_1^{-1} = \mathcal {E}_1^{-1}\mathcal {A}_1$$\end{document}A1=A1E1-1=E1-1A1 is dissipative (as a restriction of a dissipative operator) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda I_{\mathcal {X}^1}-\mathcal {A}_1$$\end{document}λIX1-A1 is surjective (since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\omega ,\infty )\subseteq \rho (\mathcal {E},\mathcal {A})=\rho (I_{\mathcal {X}^1}, \mathcal {A}_1)$$\end{document}(ω,∞)⊆ρ(E,A)=ρ(IX1,A1)). Hence, using the Lumer-Phillips theorem one obtains the same outcome. This result is similar to [7, Sect. IV].
